## CHAPITRE 2

Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides face à divers facteurs de stress environnementaux [SOURCE: Page_48]

CHAPITRE 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides face à divers facteurs de stress environnementaux [SOURCE: Page_48]

© Peter Hillman [SOURCE: Page_48]

30 [SOURCE: Page_48]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_49]

Le chapitre 2 s’intéresse à la comparaison de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides à différents facteurs environnementaux de stress à partir de données issues de la littérature. Ce travail bibliographique, réalisé sous la forme d’une méta-analyse, fait l’objet d’un article publié dans la revue European Journal of Soil Biology, intitulé « A meta-analysis to compare the sensitivities of earthworms and enchytraeids to different stressors ». Le European Journal of Soil Biology se concentre sur l'étude de la biologie et de l'écologie des organismes du sol, soulignant l'importance de la biodiversité du sol pour le bon fonctionnement des écosystèmes. La revue vise à approfondir la compréhension des interactions biologiques au sein du sol et de leur influence sur les fonctions écosystémiques. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Résumé en français Les vers de terre et les enchytréides sont des organismes du sol impliqués dans des fonctions essentielles, telles que le recyclage de la matière organique et la structure du sol, à différentes échelles. Dans les sols naturels, ces organismes sont exposés à divers facteurs abiotiques (par exemple, le climat, l'utilisation des terres et la gestion) et sont souvent utilisés comme bioindicateurs des perturbations humaines, en particulier du stress chimique. Cependant, la sensibilité de ces deux groupes d'Oligochaeta (Annelida) à différents stress n'a jamais été comparée. En utilisant des données provenant de 49 publications et 330 observations, nous avons réalisé une méta-analyse pour comparer la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides à tous types de stress dans des conditions de test similaires. Les vers de terre et les enchytréides se sont avérés également sensibles aux stress chimiques (taille d'effet moyenne −0,61 [-2,53 ; 1,30]), quel que soit le paramètre étudié (mortalité ou reproduction). La plupart des observations portaient sur les effets des pesticides (42 % des études) et des métaux lourds (40 %) sur les deux organismes. Aucune différence de sensibilité n'a été révélée lorsque ces deux stress ont été considérés séparément. En ce qui concerne les deux espèces d'enchytréides et de vers de terre les plus étudiées, les tailles d'effet moyens de toutes les combinaisons possibles d'Eisenia fetida (41 % des études), Eisenia andrei (48 %), Enchytraeus crypticus (73 %), Enchytraeus albidus (27 %) n'ont pas révélé de différences de sensibilité. Cette étude met également en évidence le manque d'études sur les espèces d'enchytréides et de vers de terre représentatives des sols naturels. Nous avons également constaté que ce sont principalement les écotoxicologues qui ont entrepris de comparer la sensibilité de ces deux organismes du sol majeurs alors qu’ils sont confrontés à d'autres facteurs de stress cruciaux, tels que les pratiques agricoles et les changements climatiques. [SOURCE: Page_49]

31 [SOURCE: Page_49]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_50]

A meta-analysis to compare the sensitivities of earthworms and enchytraeids to different stressors. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Cécile Serbource^1*, Lucas Petit-Dit-Grezeriat^2, Céline Pelosi^1 1 Institut National de La Recherche Agronomique Centre de Recherche D'Avignon, France 2 Université D'Avignon et des Pays de Vaucluse, France [SOURCE: Page_50]

*Corresponding author: C. Serbource, INRAE, Avignon Université, UMR EMMAH, F-84000, Avignon, France. 228 route de l'Aérodrome, CS 40 509, 84 914 AVIGNON Cedex 9, France. Email: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_50]

Abstract Earthworms and enchytraeids are soil organisms involved in key soil functions, such as organic matter turnover and soil structure, at different scales. In natural soils, these organisms are exposed and sensitive to different abiotic factors (e.g., climate, land use and management) and are often used as bioindicators of human disturbances, particularly chemical stress. However, the sensitivity of these two groups of Oligochaeta (Annelida) to different stressors has never been compared. Using data from 49 publications and 330 observations, we performed a meta- analysis to compare the sensitivity of earthworms and enchytraeids to all kinds of stressors under similar test conditions. Earthworms and enchytraeids were found to be equally sensitive to chemical stressors (mean effect size −0.61 [-2.53; 1.30]) regardless of the studied endpoint (mortality or reproduction). Most of the observations dealt with the effects of pesticides (42 % of studies) and heavy metals (40 %) on both organisms. No difference in sensitivity was revealed when these two stressors were considered separately. Regarding the two most studied species of enchytraeids and earthworms, the mean effect sizes of all the possible combinations of Eisenia fetida (41 % of the studies), Eisenia andrei (48 %), Enchytraeus crypticus (73 % of the studies), Enchytraeus albidus (27 %) did not reveal any differences in sensitivity. This study also highlights the lack of studies on enchytraeid and earthworm species representative of natural soils. We also revealed that mostly ecotoxicologists have compared the sensitivities of these two key soil organisms when they are exposed to and threatened by other important factors, such as agricultural practices and climate change. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Keywords: Ecotoxicological endpoints; multiple stressors; soil engineers; indicators; LC50. [SOURCE: Page_50]

32 [SOURCE: Page_50]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_51]

1. Introduction Earthworms and enchytraeids are macro- and meso-organisms, respectively, that perform several key functions in soils. At different spatial and temporal scales, both taxa are involved in litter fragmentation and organic matter decomposition (Marinissen et Didden, 1997, Brown et al., 2000), soil nutrient dynamics (Lavelle et al., 1998; Cole et al., 2002b), soil formation (Lavelle et al., 1998, Topoliantz et al., 2000), soil structure dynamics via bioporosity (van Vliet et al., 1993, Edwards et Bohlen 1996), and regulation of microbial biomass (Hedlund et Augustsson, 1995, Brown et al., 2000). Because they play key roles in the functioning of agroecosystems and are abundant in many ecosystems, earthworms and enchytraeids are both used as bioindicators of land use, agricultural practices and chemical stress (OECD, 1984, Paoletti, 1999, Didden et Römbke, 2001, Pelosi et Römbke, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_51]

The ecological niches and behaviours of these two groups do not necessarily overlap (Didden et al., 1997): For example, the earthworm Lumbricus terrestris creates extensive burrows that improve water infiltration. In contrast, enchytraeids, such as Enchytraeus albidus, play a crucial role in nutrient cycling by decomposing finer organic materials in the soil, their activities are more concentrated in the upper soil layers and leaf litter. Therefore, they can provide complementary functions in soils and complementary information on soil quality (Postma- Blaauw et al., 2012). Under natural conditions, both earthworms and enchytraeids face stressors of different origins. In agricultural soils, tillage or chemical inputs such as pesticides may harm these soil organisms (van Capelle et al., 2012, Beketov et al., 2013). Moreover, climate change has increased the vulnerability of soils and terrestrial biodiversity (IPCC 2022). Phillips et al. (2019) showed that climate variables were more important than soil properties or habitat cover in shaping earthworm communities. Drought periods and intense precipitation events now occur more frequently and cause dramatic disruptions to soil biological, physical and chemical parameters (Bellard et al., 2012). Thus, the extreme variations in soil moisture as well as the exposure to soil tillage and high quantities of chemicals—directly applied to the soil surface— can disturb these organisms, their metabolism and the interactions between them (Mäder et al., 2002, Bengtsson et al., 2005). [SOURCE: Page_51]

Earthworms and enchytraeids are both soft-bodied soil invertebrates, and enchytraeids are considered the closest relatives to earthworms according to Erséus and Källersjö (2004). Enchytraeids have been compared to other soil meso-organisms, such as springtails (Uvarov et Karaban, 2015, Hlavkova et al., 2020) or nematodes (Hagner et al., 2008), more often than to [SOURCE: Page_51]

33 [SOURCE: Page_51]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_52]

earthworms. Moreover, in some studies, different endpoints have been assessed for enchytraeids and earthworms to obtain additional data on the effects of stressors (Niva et al., 2021); therefore, the sensitivities of these two organismal groups cannot be compared. Among the studies that have properly compared earthworms and enchytraeids, some have shown that enchytraeid species exhibit a lower sensitivity than earthworms to certain chemicals and climatic parameters. In Bart et al. (2017), earthworms experienced a decrease in biomass, and they avoided soil contaminated with a copper fungicide at a lower concentration than enchytraeids. Holmstrup et al. (2022) noted that, in Arctic areas, the enchytraeid E. albidus individuals had superior freezing tolerance than earthworms. Moreover, Sverdrup et al. (2002) reported that Enchytraeus crypticus exhibited a lower sensitivity to polycyclic aromatic compound exposure than the earthworm Dendrobaena veneta; however, their respective sensitivities can vary according to the compound itself. In contrast, enchytraeids reportedly exhibit a greater sensitivity to pesticides (e.g., Sechi et al., 2014 – insecticide alpha- cypermethrin) and pig manure (Segat et al., 2020) than earthworms. Finally, Römbke and Moser (2002), based on the results of an international ring test involving 29 institutions from 15 European countries, noted that there was no clear answer as to the sensitivity of the species tested (Eisenia fetida/Eisenia andrei or E. albidus) to two chemicals (the fungicide carbendazim and the industrial chemical 4-nitrophenol). The authors concluded that there was no "most sensitive species”. Other studies comparing earthworms to enchytraeids have shown similar sensitivities between both soil organisms through a wide range of stress factors, such as uranium (Caetano et al., 2014), electrical conductivity (Gainer et al., 2019), polycyclic aromatic compounds (Kobetičovà et al., 2008) and lindane, an insecticide (Lock et al., 2002). A report published in 2009 by Jarratt and Thompson showed mixed conclusions on the sensitivities of enchytraeid and earthworm species to chemicals, although they suggested that the mortality test results indicate that Enchytraeidae species may be less sensitive than earthworms. They reported a lower sensitivity for enchytraeids in some cases but noted that the current data do not show consistent differences in sensitivity between Lumbricidae and Enchytraeidae in the laboratory or under semifield/field conditions. To summarize, the message is not clear, perhaps due to the non-quantitative nature of all these assessments. [SOURCE: Page_52]

The aim of this study was to quantify the relative sensitivities of earthworms and enchytraeids to all kinds of stressors (i.e., any factor likely to negatively affect earthworms and enchytraeids at any level of biological organization). For that purpose, we performed a meta-analysis based on an exhaustive bibliographical review that included all the studies using both earthworms and [SOURCE: Page_52]

34 [SOURCE: Page_52]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_53]

enchytraeids under the same test conditions (e.g., same soil, tested stressor). We determined the difference in sensitivity to specific stressors according to the following assumptions: (H$_1$) enchytraeids are generally less sensitive than earthworms to different stressors, (H$_2$) differences in sensitivity are mainly observed when mortality is assessed compared to reproduction or avoidance, and (H$_3$) the relative sensitivities of both groups of organisms vary according to the tested stressor and the considered species. Our findings highlight the specific gaps in the literature regarding the sensitivity of these organisms to different stressors, emphasizing the need for future research to address these gaps. [SOURCE: Page_53]

2. Methods 2.1. Literature search and inclusion/exclusion criteria A systematic literature review was conducted in January 2023 to find publications dealing with earthworms and enchytraeids exposed to different stressors. The literature search was carried out on the basis of keywords in the ISI Web of Knowledge, using the ‘‘All Databases’’ option, with the following scheme: ‘(enchytr* OR potworm* OR achaet* OR bryodril* OR buchlol* OR cernosvitoviel* OR cognat* OR frideric* OR guaranidril* OR lumbricil* OR hemienchytr* OR oconnorel* OR hemifrideric* OR henl* OR marionin* OR mesenchytr*) and (earthworm* OR lumbric* OR aporrect* OR eisen* OR dendrob* OR alloloboph* OR octalas* OR dendrodril* OR diporodril* OR eophil* OR helodril* OR kritodril* OR octodril* OR prosellodril* OR scherothec* OR satchell* OR proctodril* orodril* OR postandril* OR perelia* OR andrei* OR fetid* OR foetid*)’ in Topics. From a total of 967 references, a first selection was made using titles and abstracts. The full texts were examined when the information was considered consistent for the meta-analysis. To complete the search, bibliographic reviews that included both annelids were selected, and references were carefully examined to extract any new articles that could be relevant for the meta-analysis. [SOURCE: Page_53]

We considered only publications providing data on terrestrial earthworms and enchytraeids, all species included. For instance, papers dealing with aquatic and bioluminescent worm species were excluded (Rodionova et al., 2017). The data on earthworms and enchytraeids had to be from the same study to compare the sensitivity of these groups under the same conditions (e.g., active substance, type of substrate). Moreover, we did not consider studies dealing with chemical bioaccumulation in animals, as bioaccumulation cannot be easily correlated with certain factors (Tosza et al., 2010; Santorufo et al., 2012). We selected only the studies where the tested factors were supposed to be harmful to soil organisms, specifically those detrimental [SOURCE: Page_53]

35 [SOURCE: Page_53]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_54]

to their life cycle parameters (e.g., survival, growth, fecundity, juvenile survival). We did not consider studies on biochar addition (possibly beneficial to them, e.g., Briones et al., 2020) or on tillage (Engell et al., 2021) because these factors can be beneficial to enchytraeid populations (e.g., Pelosi et Römbke, 2016). Regarding organic and inorganic waste, one paper was kept because it focused on contaminated incineration ash (Kobetičová et al., 2010). The final set was composed of 49 publications (i.e., 48 papers and one PhD thesis), which included 330 lines in the database (Annexe A$_2$.1). [SOURCE: Page_54]

2.2. Data extraction For each of the 49 studies, the following data were recorded (Annexe A$_2$.1): -   author, date of publication, -   stressor type (i.e., pesticides, metals, wastes, others); -   stressor subcategory (i.e., for pesticides: fungicide, herbicide and insecticide; for metals: antimony, barium, beryllium, cadmium, chromium, copper, lanthanum, manganese, mercury, molybdenum, nickel, lead, silver, uranium and zinc; for wastes: inorganic waste, organic waste, vinasse, pig manure waste, mineral byproducts, and dredged sediment; for others: pesticide additive, boric acid, electrical conductivity, linear alkylbenzene sulfonate, chlorinated paraffins and polycyclic aromatic compounds); -   earthworm species (i.e., Aporrectodea caliginosa, Aporrectodea longa, Dendrobaena veneta, Eisenia andrei and Eisenia fetida) and enchytraeid species (i.e., Enchytraeus albidus, Enchytraeus crypticus and Enchytraeus luxurious); -   the observed variable (i.e., survival, reproduction, avoidance, growth, population abundance) and its measured endpoint (LCx: lethal concentration; ECx: effect concentration; NOEC: no observed effect concentration; NEC: no effect concentration; and LOEC: lowest observed effect concentration); -   the mean value of the endpoints with the standard error, standard deviation, or the respective 95 % confidence intervals; -   and the number of replicates. [SOURCE: Page_54]

As the choice was made to select studies that provided ecotoxicological endpoint values to ease the comparison between earthworms and enchytraeids, all the selected studies were laboratory experiments. First, the data were collected from available tables; otherwise, the data were collected from the text. Searching for data in the figures (graphs) was not ruled out, but none of the data matched [SOURCE: Page_54]

36 [SOURCE: Page_54]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_55]

our search criteria. Because of compliance with ISO standards (ISO, 1998, OECD, 2015), some methodological differences were tolerated when comparing data for earthworms and enchytraeids. For example, in reproduction tests, cocoons or juveniles were counted for earthworms, while the number of juveniles was counted for enchytraeids. The duration of the experiment was also not the same for standard tests of earthworms and enchytraeids, i.e., 21 and 42 days, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_55]

For both earthworms and enchytraeids, when the endpoint value was not given as a precise number but was bounded by > or <, the data were adjusted as follows: in the case of LC50 > x, the data were not considered to indicate that the sensitivity of the organism would be overestimated by considering x as the LC50 value. In the case of LC50 < x, the x value was used, and it is conservative to consider x as the LC50 value. This latter case represented 20 of the 330 observations in the database (i.e., 6 % of the data). [SOURCE: Page_55]

Four comparisons involving boric acid were found in two studies (Becker et al., 2011, Niemeyer et al., 2018). Becker et al. (2011) characterized boric acid as a fertilizer and fungicide, but Niemeyer et al. (2018) did not mention any role or expected effect. Boric acid can be used as an insecticide, acaricide, herbicide, or fungicide (Büyükgüzel et al., 2013). Since it can be placed in different subcategories (insecticide, fungicide, and fertilizer), it was placed into the "others" category. [SOURCE: Page_55]

2.3. Effect size calculation When the standard deviation (SD) was not given directly from a table, it was calculated with the t distribution for small samples (n <60) using the following formula (Higgins and Green, 2011): 𝑢𝑝𝑝𝑒𝑟 𝑙𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑡 𝐶𝐼 − 𝑙𝑜𝑤𝑒𝑟 𝑙𝑖𝑚𝑖𝑡 𝐶𝐼 𝑆𝐷 = √𝑛 ∗ 𝑡𝑎𝑙𝑝ℎ𝑎,𝑑𝑓 ∗ 2 [SOURCE: Page_55]

With talpha,df, the T.INV.2T functions in syntax with two associated arguments: probability, associated with Student's t-distribution (here, the alpha=0.05 to respect the 95 % CI from extracted data), and degrees of freedom (equal to the group sample size - 1). When neither the 95 % CI nor the SD were given, we used the worst-case scenario, i.e., the highest SD calculated for the same endpoint in the database (LC10, LC50, EC10, EC20, EC50). NOEC and LOEC values have no variance, so their standard deviation (SD) was set to zero. When the number of replicates was not the same between the treatments and the control, the [SOURCE: Page_55]

37 [SOURCE: Page_55]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_56]

lower number was considered to be conservative (i.e., less weight was given to the study). When the number of replicates was not given and the tests referred to ISO standards, we considered the number of replicates advised in official procedures (e.g., ISO, 1998, OECD, 2015). The confidence intervals (CIs) for each estimate of effect size were calculated using bias-corrected bootstrapping methods, which corrects for nonnormality of the data and nonconstant standard errors (Efron and Hastie, 2016). Hedge's d is a standardized mean difference that accounts for the potential bias in estimating population effect size (Hedges and Olkin, 1985). The magnitude of the difference between earthworm and enchytraeid sensitivities was calculated with the Hedge metric using the natural logarithm of the response ratio (LRR) as follows (van Groenigen et al., 2014): 𝑥𝑖 𝐿𝑅𝑅 = 𝑙𝑛( ) 𝑥𝑢 [SOURCE: Page_56]

with xi and xu being the values of the enchytraeid and earthworm endpoints, respectively. Therefore, when the LRR equals zero, the two organisms have the same sensitivity. The LRR variance (V) was calculated as follows (van Groenigen et al., 2014): [SOURCE: Page_56]

1  𝑆𝐷𝑖 2 1     𝑆𝐷𝑢 2 𝑉 = ∗( ) +     ∗(     ) 𝑛𝑖  𝑥𝑖   𝑛𝑢     𝑥𝑢 [SOURCE: Page_56]

where xi and xu are the mean values of the endpoints for earthworms and enchytraeids, respectively; 𝑆𝐷𝑖 and 𝑆𝐷𝑢 are the standard deviations of the endpoint values for enchytraeid and earthworm, respectively; and 𝑛𝑖 and 𝑛𝑢 are the sample sizes in the enchytraeid and earthworm tests, respectively. Finally, effect sizes were weighted by the inverse of their variance in all the models, giving more weight to well-replicated studies (Koricheva et al., 2013). [SOURCE: Page_56]

3. Meta-analysis 3.1. General modelling approach Every model incorporated a random effect associated with the study's identity to accommodate correlated data arising from the same study (Viechtbauer, 2010). The models were selected using the Akaike information criterion (AIC). This criterion represents a compromise between bias decreasing with the number of free parameters and parsimony, the desire to describe the data with as few parameters as possible. Lower AIC values indicate a better-fit model; therefore, for each calculation, the smallest AIC was considered for the LRR calculation. Random effect models were tested for the lines (to allow the real effect to vary from one dataset to another), [SOURCE: Page_56]

38 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_57]

the authors (to allow the actual effect to vary from one article to another), and a combination of the two (Borenstein et al., 2010). A combination of the two modalities was associated with a lower AIC for every case, and these models were systematically used. For each model, we used the restricted maximum likelihood (REML) method, which aims to provide unbiased estimates of variance components by accounting for fixed effects in the model. All analyses were performed with R Software (R Development Core Team, 2014) using the metafor package (Viechtbauer, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_57]

3.2. Analysis First, a meta-analysis was carried out using estimated overall effect size in a multivariate linear model, calculated using the rma. mv function from the metafor package in R (Viechtbauer, 2010). In the context of a meta-analysis, the effect size (with its 95 % confidence interval) is a standardized measure that quantifies the magnitude of the treatment effect or association across all included studies. Then, the logarithm of the response ratio (LRR) was calculated to explore, throught earthworm and enchytraeid endpoints, the results of this model on all the data, all the factors and all the species combined. This main model enabled us to test our first assumption (H$_1$). [SOURCE: Page_57]

We also compared earthworm and enchytraeid data by splitting them according to the studied endpoints (i.e., reproduction or survival rate) to test the second assumption (H$_2$). We further investigated the effects of the different stressors (H$_3$) on the response ratio (LRR) by stressor category, a moderator with 4 levels: pesticides, metals, wastes, and others. We finally investigated the potential "species effect" of the most represented earthworm and enchytraeid species (H$_3$), using the response ratio of the earthworm species categorical moderator at 2 levels (i.e., E. andrei and E. fetida, which accounted for 48 % and 41 %, respectively; Annexe A$_2$.1), coupled with the enchytraeid species categorical moderator at 2 levels (i.e., E. albidus and E. crypticus, which accounted for 27 % and 73 %, respectively; Annexe A$_2$.1). [SOURCE: Page_57]

Publication bias was assessed by Egger's regression test for funnel plot asymmetry between the standard normal deviation of the effect sizes and precision (i.e., inverse of their standard errors). We retained the multilevel linear model rma. mv by adding a standard error moderator. This test is powerful given the fairly large number of studies. The findings demonstrated the absence of publication bias: we detected no significant asymmetry (z-value = -0.62; p value = 0.53) in the funnel plot (Annexe A$_2$.3), as none of the intercepts from Egger's regression exhibited a [SOURCE: Page_57]

39 [SOURCE: Page_57]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_58]

significant deviation from zero. [SOURCE: Page_58]

### 4. Results 4.1. Data description

Figure 12. Relative coverage of different stressors tested for the earthworms and enchytraeids sensitivity comparisons, in 330 observations from 49 studies. Sunburst diagram representing proportions of observations for each category. [SOURCE: Page_58]

The 49 selected studies addressed a total of 30 different stressors (Figure 12) classified into four different categories. “Metals” accounted for 39.7 % of the observations, with the most represented being molybdenum (2 studies, 36 observations) and zinc (2 studies, 19 observations). “Pesticides” represented 25.5 %, with subcategories including insecticides (13 studies, 52 observations), fungicides (6 studies, 31 observations) and herbicides (1 study, 1 observation). Among all the observations, 14.2 % were classified into the “Wastes” category, which is widely represented by vinasse (1 study, 26 observations). Finally, “Others” accounted for 20.6 % of the observations, with polycyclic aromatic compounds (2 studies, 30 [SOURCE: Page_58]

40 [SOURCE: Page_58]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_59]

observations) and pesticide additives (1 study, 22 observations) being the most represented. In total, 98.8% of the data concerned contaminant tests (all the stressors studied except for the 4 observations for the electrical conductivity), even though no restrictions were imposed during the literature search. [SOURCE: Page_59]

The 49 selected studies reported reproduction endpoints in 71.2 % of the cases (43 studies, 235 observations), survival in 20.9 % (20 studies, 69 observations), avoidance in 4.54 % (3 studies, 15 observations), growth in 2.4 % (1 study, 8 observations), and population abundance in 1% (1 study, 3 observations), with EC50 (106 observations) and NOEC (68 observations) calculations dominating (Table 1). [SOURCE: Page_59]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_60]

4.2. Meta-analysis results When considering all the stressors together, earthworms and enchytraeids were found to be equally sensitive, as the mean effect size was -0.61 [-2.53, 1.31] (Figure 13a). The results were considered non-significant because the confidence interval crosses zero, indicating no statistically significant effect. Similarly, exploration of the data across the four stressor types yielded nonsignificant results (Figure 13b), with each confidence interval intersecting zero. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Figure 13. Log ratio of the mean effect size (in bold) and its 95 % confidence interval (in square brackets) for (a) all stressors, and (b) the different stressor types separately: pesticides, metals, wastes and others stressors. The number of observations and papers are into round brackets. Error bars correspond to 95 % confidence intervals computed either from the standard deviations reported by the authors when available (292 data for earthworm; 293 data for enchytraeids) or from the standard deviation using the worst-case scenario (38 data for earthworms; 37 data for enchytraeids). On the left of the 0: higher sensitivity of enchytraeids; on the right: higher sensitivity of earthworms. [SOURCE: Page_60]

Similarly, when the data on mortality (i.e., LC10, LC50, and some NOEC and LOEC data) and those on reproduction (i.e., some EC10, EC20, EC25, EC50, LOEC, NEC and NOEC) were considered separately (Figure 14), no difference in sensitivity was found between earthworms and enchytraeids. [SOURCE: Page_60]

42 [SOURCE: Page_60]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_61]

Figure 14. Log ratio of the mean effect size (in bold) and its 95 % confidence interval (in square brackets) for (a) survival endpoints, and (b) reproduction endpoints. The number of observations and papers are into round brackets. Error bars correspond to 95 % confidence intervals computed either from the standard deviations reported by the authors when available (314 data for earthworm; 309 data for enchytraeids concerning reproduction data; 316 data for earthworm; 318 data for enchytraeids concerning survival data ) or from the standard deviation using the worst-case scenario (16 data for earthworms; 21 data for enchytraeids concerning reproduction data; 14 data for earthworms; 14 data for enchytraeids concerning survival data). On the left of the 0: higher sensitivity of enchytraeids; on the right: higher sensitivity of earthworms. [SOURCE: Page_61]

The sensitivities of the different species were assessed when the dataset was statistically sufficient for comparison. Therefore, E. andrei (earthworm) data were compared with E. crypticus (enchytraeid) data, E. andrei with E. albidus (enchytraeid), E. fetida (earthworm) with E. albidus and E. fetida with E. crypticus. The sensitivities of these species were similar (Annexe A$_2$.4). [SOURCE: Page_61]

5. Discussion This study allowed us to quantitatively compare the relative sensitivities of earthworms and enchytraeids to different types of stressors. On the basis of 49 published studies and 330 observations, we revealed similar sensitivities to stressors in these two soil organisms (H$_1$). The conclusion was the same when considering the different stressors, species or endpoints separately (H$_2$ and H$_3$). Although the differences were not significant, enchytraeids tended to be slightly more sensitive to metals, pesticide additives and electrical conductivity. In contrast, [SOURCE: Page_61]

43 [SOURCE: Page_61]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_62]

earthworms tended to be slightly more sensitive to pesticides regardless of their mode of action (fungicide or insecticide), but the differences were still nonsignificant. Large confidence intervals were found for all mean effect sizes. This variability was probably due to the variety of test conditions used for the observations; in particular, the tested substances revealed alternating greater sensitivities of enchytraeids or earthworms. Moreover, the high number of studies with no information on data uncertainty (e.g., standard deviation) (i.e., 58 observations) could explain the absence of significant differences since when neither the 95 % CI nor the SD was given, we used a conservative approach by giving the highest SD calculated for the same endpoint in the database. [SOURCE: Page_62]

These results are not in accordance with our assumptions that enchytraeids are less sensitive than earthworms to different stressors (H$_1$), that differences in sensitivity are mainly observed when mortality is assessed (H$_2$), and that the relative sensitivity of both groups of organisms varies according to the tested stressor and the considered species (H$_3$). Enchytraeids, which are smaller than earthworms and 80 % microbivorous and 20 % saprovorous (Didden, 1990, 1993), ingest less material and smaller particles than earthworms in soils and thus could be less exposed to chemicals (Tourinho et al., 2021, Coleman et al., 2004). However, the relationship between size and sensitivity is debatable, as earthworms are much larger than springtails but are less sensitive to all kinds of pesticides, such as formulations or active substances (Joimel et al., 2022). Moreover, in the same study (Joimel et al., 2022), differences in sensitivity were reported between springtails and enchytraeids, but both of these taxonomic groups belong to the same size class (i.e., soil mesofauna). Small animals have a greater surface area-to-volume ratio than larger organisms and are thus potentially more exposed to external stressors. Regarding chemicals, the size class would be of particular importance if the main route of exposure was dermal contact. Enchytraeids live in close contact with the soil porewater fraction, and their routes of exposure are dermal, intestinal and respiratory and earthworms one is dermal but also dietary exposure (Lock and Janssen, 2003, Römbke, 2003, Hirano and Tamae, 2011). Earthworms, which might be thought to be more sensitive to chemicals at first glance due in particular to their larger size, can ultimately be less exposed than small enchytraeids in soils. Both earthworms and enchytraeids must maintain a moist body surface to facilitate oxygen absorption through their tegument, which is highly permeable to water. Pesticide characteristics (e.g., polarity, solubility in water) influence the available concentrations in water and in soils, leading to varying sensitivities among taxonomic groups. In accordance with our results, Römbke and Moser (2002) concluded that the overall range of sensitivities of oligochaetes to [SOURCE: Page_62]

44 [SOURCE: Page_62]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_63]

chemicals was usually quite similar. These authors linked the lack of differentiation between the sensitivities of E. albidus and E. fetida exposed to carbendazim to the high toxicity of this persistent compound. More research should be dedicated to quantifying the different routes of exposure to chemicals for soil organisms to better understand their respective sensitivities in different situations. Moreover, both earthworms and enchytraeids can be considered in risk assessments, and while earthworms are most commonly used, there are many reasons to support the use of enchytraeids in testing. Enchytraeids can thrive in soils where earthworms are scarce (Jarratt and Thompson, 2009). Moreover, Römbke et al. (1994) encouraged the use of enchytraeids when comparing laboratory results with those obtained under semifield and field conditions, as the same species (or closely related species) can be used at all test levels. Other practical and ecological points (ease of handling and rearing, short generation time, reduced soil requirements, cost-effectiveness) make them good organisms for inclusion in laboratory experiments (Römbke and Moser, 2002). [SOURCE: Page_63]

Among the tested species, the earthworm Eisenia sp. represented 89 % of all species included in the selected studies. E. fetida and E. andrei are composting worms that are not representative of mineral soils and are less sensitive to pesticides than species found in cultivated fields (Pelosi et al., 2013). However, these species are recommended as standard test organisms in various guidelines, such as those of the OECD (OECD, 1984) and ISO (ISO, 1993, 1998, 2019), and have been widely used in published ecotoxicological studies, mainly because they are easy to rear. A few studies have used species that are found in agricultural soils. In Bart et al. (2017), the LC50 and EC50 values were systematically lower for A. caliginosa (earthworm) than for E. albidus (enchytraeid) (Annexe A$_2$.1). In Holmstrup and Krogh (2001), the opposite occurred, with systematically higher LC50 and EC50 values for A. caliginosa and A. longa (earthworm) than for E. albidus (Annexe A$_2$.1). Thus, the results should be contrasted according to the tested stressor, and additional data are needed to assess the sensitivity of earthworms and enchytraeid species commonly found in the field. [SOURCE: Page_63]

The choice was made here to select studies that provided ecotoxicological endpoint values to facilitate comparisons between earthworms and enchytraeids. Potentially, interesting studies have been excluded that compared both organisms. For instance, Amossé et al. (2016) assessed the short-term effects of two fungicides (Cuprafor Micro® made of copper oxychloride and Swing Gold® made of epoxiconazole and dimoxystrobin) on enchytraeid and earthworm communities under field conditions. They found greater negative effects on the diversity and [SOURCE: Page_63]

45 [SOURCE: Page_63]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_64]

community structure of earthworms than on those of enchytraeids. Similarly, Pereira et al. (2019) studied the degree to which seawater intrusion and irrigation threaten biodiversity via a terrestrial model ecosystem (TME) approach. They found that the abundance of only enchytraeids significantly decreased, although earthworms generally suffered. These field or semifield studies allowed us to investigate the sensitivity of several species found in natural soils under realistic conditions, which offers more comprehensive insight into the ecological dynamics of the systems (Wall and Moore, 1999). TMEs, which were proposed in the 1990s, are useful experimental systems for assessing the impact of factors such as pollution or climate change on terrestrial ecosystems. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Although the literature search was performed to include all kinds of stressors in this study, the great majority of the articles in our final selection concerned chemicals. Very few data are available on the effects of climatic parameters on soil organisms (Classen et al., 2015), and data comparing their responses to these parameters remain scarce (Otomo, 2011). However, earthworms and enchytraeids, in addition to all other soil organisms, are increasingly having to cope with climate change (Pritchard, 2011). Warming, changes in precipitation regimes, and changes in the quantity and quality of soil carbon inputs can disrupt the habitat and food resources of soil-living organisms, affecting their survival and reproductive capabilities (Maraldo and Holmstrup, 2009). Some species, such as the enchytraeid Cognettia sphagnetorum, which has strong potential to recover from severe drought stress events, appear to have adaptive potential to climate parameters (Maraldo and Holmstrup, 2009). They can vertically migrate as a strategy for evading dry surface layers and high temperatures (Springett et al., 1970). However, these organisms were found to have no genetic adaptation to drought, as drought-exposed populations of enchytraeids had the same high sensitivity as those from unexposed control plots (Maraldo, 2009). E. albidus has a high osmotic pressure on its body fluids and can live in environments with strong fluctuations in moisture (Maraldo, 2009), which also enables Fridericia galba to survive in soil with a water retention capacity of less than 20 % (corresponding to values below -9.8bar) for more than 49 days (Dózsa-Farkas 1973). However, additional research is needed to understand the physiological responses of enchytraeids to desiccation. Earthworms are known to be sensitive to drought and high temperatures (Bayley et al., 2010; Lima et al., 2015; González-Alcaraz and van Gestel, 2016), but they have adaptive strategies such as diapause or other physiological mechanisms, such as an increase in the free amino acid alanine, to survive long periods of drought (Holmstrup et al., 2016; Jiménez and Decaëns, 2004). Thus, in the actual context of climatic disturbances and [SOURCE: Page_64]

46 [SOURCE: Page_64]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_65]

knowing the potential effects of combined stressors on soil organisms (e.g., Bindesbøl et al., 2005; Jensen et al., 2009; Singh et al., 2019), the equilibrium between species and taxa could be modified in natural ecosystems in the future. Maraldo et al. (2006) noted that the synergistic effects of climate change factors and environmental contamination on enchytraeids have been insufficiently explored. Further studies should assess the effects of climatic parameters on functionally useful soil organisms and their relative sensitivity to desiccation or heat in realistic contexts. [SOURCE: Page_65]

We also know that enchytraeids are tolerant of soil acidity (Römbke, 1991), whereas earthworm abundance and diversity are generally low in acidic soils (Chan and Mead, 2003, Moore et al., 2013, Wu and al., 2020). Enchytraeids exhibit high tolerance to other soil properties (clay and organic matter contents) (Kuperman et al., 2006; van Gestel et al., 2011, Castro-Ferreira et al., 2012) and temperature (Holmstrup et al., 2022). However, once again, there is no real comparison to date. The combined effects of stressors related to soil properties and climate were examined by Yli-Olli and Huhta (2000), who assessed the co-occurrence of soil pH, moisture and resource addition on Dendrobaena octaedra (Lumbricidae) and Cognettia sphagnetorum (Enchytraeidae). They found that D. octaedra exhibited the highest biomass in spruce stand humus at higher pH values, while C. sphagnetorum thrived best at higher moisture levels. [SOURCE: Page_65]

6. Conclusion For the first time, this exhaustive synthesis provides a quantitative comparison of the sensitivity of earthworms versus enchytraeids to extrinsic factors (such as metals and pesticides) potentially affecting soil organisms. Under laboratory conditions, comparable sensitivities were found for earthworms and enchytraeids regardless of the studied species, or endpoint. Therefore, to test the effects of metals or pesticides, we could recommend testing on one of these groups in order to limit costs and labour. However, for other stressors such as climate parameters and for a thorough understanding of soil functioning, it remains crucial to consider the physiological responses of both groups due to their distinct ecological roles and potential sensitivity difference. Moreover, to fully capture the complexity of natural habitats, it would be relevant to use species inhabiting mineral soils instead of model species commonly found in organic rich habitats. For decades, researchers have been deepening their understanding of the impacts of natural and anthropic stressors on soil fauna and their related functions, which has allowed to reveal the importance of maintaining the presence and function of biodiversity in [SOURCE: Page_65]

47 [SOURCE: Page_65]

Chapitre 2 – Analyse comparative de la sensibilité des vers de terre et des enchytréides [SOURCE: Page_66]

our soils. Let’s keep digging in that direction. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Acknowledgements The authors thank the PACA region and INRAE AgroEcoSystem Department for PhD funding. The authors also thank David Makowski and Guillaume Cinkus for useful advice with the R scripts. [SOURCE: Page_66]

48 [SOURCE: Page_66]

## CHAPITRE 3

Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse : Impact sur les caractéristiques physiologiques, les traits d’histoire de vie et le comportement [SOURCE: Page_67]

CHAPITRE 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse : Impact sur les caractéristiques physiologiques, les traits d’histoire de vie et le comportement [SOURCE: Page_67]

Libre de droit [SOURCE: Page_67]

49 [SOURCE: Page_67]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_68]

La méta-analyse a révélé un manque significatif d’études sur les enchytréides en lien avec les facteurs des changements climatiques. Une étude a été menée au département des Écosciences de l’Université d’Aarhus, au Danemark au sein du « Climate Stress lab ». Ce groupe de recherche, dirigé par le professeur Martin Holmstrup et le professeur associé Stine Slotsbo, étudie l'écophysiologie et l'écotoxicologie des invertébrés terrestres, en mettant l'accent sur l'adaptation au stress climatique et chimique. Les recherches du groupe visent à comprendre les mécanismes physiologiques et moléculaires à l'origine des adaptations au stress chez divers invertébrés ectothermes, notamment les vers de terre, les enchytréides, les collemboles, les acariens et les limaces. Le chapitre 3 présente une étude expérimentale sur les effets de la sécheresse sur les traits d’histoire de vie et le comportement de l’enchytréide Enchytraeus albidus. Ce travail sera soumis à la revue Applied Soil Ecology début 2025, intitulé « Responses of Enchytraeus albidus (Clitellata: Enchytraeidae) to changes in soil water potential ». Cette revue s’intéresse aux études portant sur l’implication des organismes du sol dans la santé et la fertilité des sols et la durabilité, à la fois dans les environnements gérés par l'homme (systèmes agricoles et forestiers) et dans les environnements (semi-)naturels. [SOURCE: Page_68]

Résumé en français Les enchytréides sont des organismes largement répandus dans les sols et confrontés à des menaces croissantes liées aux changements climatiques. Cette étude a examiné l'impact du stress hydrique sur les paramètres de leur cycle de vie et leur comportement, cherchant à déterminer si les enchytréides interrompent toutes leurs fonctions en même temps ou s'ils en priorisent certaines lorsque les sols s'assèchent. Une gamme de teneurs en eau du sol a été créée, allant de 6 à 24 % de teneur en eau massique du sol, correspondant à des potentiels hydriques du sol entre -236 kPa et -1,80 kPa. Diverses expériences ont évalué les effets de ces niveaux d'humidité sur l'évitement comportemental, l'activité d'alimentation, la teneur en eau corporelle, la survie et la reproduction. Des conditions optimales d'humidité, représentées par un niveau de contrôle de - 2,57 kPa, ont permis une survie de 100 %, une teneur élevée en eau du corps, une activité d'alimentation optimale et le taux de reproduction le plus élevé. Des conditions légèrement plus humides (-2,17 kPa) étaient également favorables. Cependant, à mesure que l'humidité du sol diminuait, des impacts significatifs sont apparus : à partir de -4,83 kPa, l'activité d'alimentation a chuté de manière marquée, révélant une plus grande sensibilité de l’indicateur lié à l'alimentation qu’à reproduction. Bien que les enchytréides aient maintenu leur teneur en eau corporelle au sein des limites considérées comme optimales et qu’ils aient survécu dans des sols modérément secs, une dessiccation sévère du sol (à partir de -14,07 kPa et en 50 [SOURCE: Page_68]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_69]

dessous) a compromis les taux de survie, réduit la teneur en eau corporelle et entraîné l'arrêt de l'alimentation et de la reproduction. Dans cette expérience, les enchytréides n'ont pas évité les sols secs tant que le potentiel hydrique n'était pas inférieur à -28,79 kPa, correspondant à une teneur en eau d'environ 10 % ; à ce stade, ils ont activement évité le sol sec et l’étude a révélé des taux de mortalité élevés (74%). Cette étude souligne l'importance de comprendre les adaptations physiologiques des enchytréides et leurs interactions écologiques pour une gestion efficace des sols, ainsi que pour développer des stratégies visant à atténuer les effets négatifs des changements climatiques sur les communautés de la faune du sol. [SOURCE: Page_69]

51 [SOURCE: Page_69]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_70]

Responses of Enchytraeus albidus (Clitellata: Enchytraeidae) to changes in soil water potential [SOURCE: Page_70]

Cécile Serbource^1,∗ , Martin Holmstrup 2 , Stine Slotsbo^2 , Céline Pelosi^1 [SOURCE: Page_70]

1 INRAE, Avignon Université, UMR EMMAH, F-84000, Avignon, France 2 Aarhus University, Department of Ecoscience, C.F. Møllers Alle 4, Building 1120, 8000 Aarhus C, Denmark [SOURCE: Page_70]

*Corresponding author: C. Serbource, INRAE, Avignon Université, UMR EMMAH, F-84000, Avignon, France. [SOURCE: Page_70]

228 route de l'Aérodrome, CS 40 509, 84 914 AVIGNON Cedex 9, France. email: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_70]

Abstract Enchytraeids (’pot worms’ or ‘white worms’) are widespread soil-dwelling organisms facing increasing threats from climate changes. This study investigated how drought stress affects their life cycle parameters and behavior, aiming to determine whether enchytraeids halt all functions simultaneously or prioritize some over others when soils dry out. A series of soil water contents was created, ranging from 6 % to 24 % of dry weight, corresponding to soil water potentials from -236 kPa to -1.80 kPa. Various experiments assessed the effects of these moisture levels on behavioral avoidance, feeding activity, body water content, survival and reproduction. Optimal moisture conditions, represented by a control moisture level of -2.57 kPa, ensured 100 % survival, high body water content, robust feeding activity, and the highest reproduction rate. Slightly wetter conditions (-2.17 kPa) were also favorable. However, as soil moisture decreased, significant impacts emerged: from -4.83 kPa onwards, feeding activity dropped markedly, revealing a greater sensitivity of the feeding-related indicator compared to reproduction. Although enchytraeids maintained body water content within the considered optimal limits and survived in moderately dry soils, severe soil desiccation (from -14.07 kPa and below) compromised survival rates, reduced body water content, and led to cessation of both feeding and reproduction. In this experiment, enchytraeids did not avoid dry soils until soil water potential dropped below -28.79 kPa, corresponding to a water content of around 10 %, at which point they actively avoided the dry soil and experienced high mortality rates (74%). This study highlights the importance of understanding the physiological adaptations of enchytraeids and their ecological interactions for effective soil management and to develop strategies aimed at mitigating the negative effects of climate changes on soil fauna communities. [SOURCE: Page_70]

Keywords: Juveniles hatching; body water content; bait lamina; avoidance; soil water content. [SOURCE: Page_70]

52 [SOURCE: Page_70]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_71]

### 1. Introduction

Enchytraeids are small annelids commonly found worldwide (Didden, 1993). They occupy various trophic niches, including epigeic, epi-endogeic, or endogeic environments, usually living in the top 5 to 10 cm of soil (Korobushkin et al., 2024). These tiny organisms, with body diameters ranging from 0.1 to 2 mm and lengths between 6–50 mm (Schmelz and Collado, 2010) were reported to be 80 % microbivorous and 20 % saprovorous (Didden, 1993). They significantly contribute to nutrient cycling and soil structure maintenance, performing functional roles similar to earthworms, for example, soil bioturbation, enhancement of plant growth, increase in soil fertility and breaking down organic material, albeit at a different scale (van Vliet et al., 1993; Cole et al., 2000b; Pelosi and Römbke, 2018; Serbource et al., 2024b). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Enchytraeids have water permeable epidermis with no particular protection against evaporation, making them vulnerable to drought and typically confined to moist habitats (Didden, 1991). Severe droughts can push these communities beyond their survival thresholds, affecting their abundance, diversity, and composition (Maraldo and Holmstrup, 2010; Nielsen, 1955a, b; Coyle et al., 2017). At water potentials of -11.0 bars (corresponding to the "permanent wilting point" of soil, where it retains only 10–15 % of its maximum moisture), the water content of E. albidus significantly decreased, resulting in approximately 50 % mortality (Maraldo et al., 2009). Beylich and Achazi (1999) found a significant decrease in reproduction at water contents below 20 % dry weight (DW). Specifically, enchytraeid offspring were reduced by 39 % at 15 % DW water content, with further decreases observed as soil moisture decreased. Interaction of factors also can worsen droughts and complicate enchytraeids survival. High temperatures increase desiccation stress (Lundkvist, 1982; Briones et al., 2007; IPCC, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_71]

Field populations of enchytraeids (Cognettia sphagnetorum) can show genetic evolutionary responses to realistic climatic changes (artificially reduced summer precipitation) over relatively short time scales (Batallion et al. 2016). However, such genetic responses did not increase drought tolerance of this species after six years of repeated removal of summer precipitation in a field experiment suggesting that physiological or behavioral strategies for drought survival are in play (Maraldo et al. 2008). Thus, enchytraeids have various strategies to cope with unfavorable moisture conditions. They can seek out more humid microhabitats by migrating to deeper soil layers (Springett et al., 1970; Didden, 1991; Maraldo, 2009) or survive in desiccation-tolerant cocoon stages during dry conditions (Christensen, 1956; Lagerlöf and Strandh, 1997). Additionally, they have been observed entering inactive states or diapause [SOURCE: Page_71]

53 [SOURCE: Page_71]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_72]

during dry periods (Willard, 1972). Certain species have adapted perfectly to survive in drier habitats, such as Fridericia galba and Enchytraeus buchholzi, which can endure arid conditions as long as temperatures are not too high (Abrahamsen, 1971; Dózsa-Farkas, 1977). Studies have shown that enchytraeid populations can thrive even in areas with very dry summers and severe winters where freezing of the soil can cause extensive dehydration of some enchytraeid species without freezing of their body fluids, indicating distinct physiological or behavioral adaptations (Willard, 1974a, b; Gröngröft and Miehlich, 1983; Pedersen and Holmstrup 2008). Drought indirectly impacts enchytraeids by altering their food resources, as it increases the microbial C/N ratio and shifts the community towards more fungi and complex substrates (Jensen et al., 2003). This forces enchytraeids to adapt to new food sources, favoring more fungivorous and desiccation-tolerant species (Sulkava et al., 1996). [SOURCE: Page_72]

To date, most studies have examined specific aspects of soil drought effects on organisms separately, such as survival, reproduction, feeding activity, or avoidance. This article presents a novel approach by simultaneously investigating how soil water content affects life history traits (survival, reproduction) and behavioral responses (feeding, avoidance) of E. albidus, offering a comprehensive perspective on the impacts of soil water potential. Using soil water potential provides the advantage of making results universally applicable across all soil types. Based on the assumption that the optimum moisture level for enchytraeids is around 20 % (which will serve as the control), we here determined the moisture threshold for negative effects on E. albidus. For that, we conducted a series of laboratory experiments involving a gradient of soil moisture, thus providing insights into soil organism resilience to climatic stress. [SOURCE: Page_72]

2. Materials and methods 2.1. Animals Enchytraeus albidus sensu strictu (Erséus et al. 2019) came from a mass culture held at Aarhus University, Denmark, where they were kept at 20 ± 0.5 °C, (12:12 h light/dark cycle) and fed weekly on ground oats. Worms were originally collected from decaying seaweed on a beach near Grettislaug (about 10 km from the town Holar), northern Iceland (65°88’N, 19°74’W). For the experiment, we selected adults (developed clitellum or visible eggs) of similar size (i.e. 20– 30 mm long, diameter 0.18–0.30 mm at clitellum). [SOURCE: Page_72]

### 2.2. Soil and range of water content

54 [SOURCE: Page_72]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_73]

A loamy sand soil was collected from a Danish agricultural field (Foulumgaard Experimental Station, 56°29’N, 9°34’E, Central Jutland region) which has been managed under organic farming for more than 20 years. It was composed of 32 % coarse sand (>200 µm), 48 % fine sand (20–200 µm), 9 % silt (2–20 µm), 7 % clay (<2 µm), 4 % organic matter, with a pH of 5.9. The soil was dried at 70 °C for 48 hours, sieved through a 2 mm mesh, and stored in closed boxes, protected from moisture gain until used in the experiments. [SOURCE: Page_73]

A gradient of 10 different moisture levels was created by adding suitable amounts of demineralized water to dry soil and ensuring thorough mixing (Table 3). The soil moisture ranged from 6 % to 24 %, corresponding to soil water content from -236 kPa to -1.80 kPa (i.e. 6 % to 24 %), following Holmstrup's (2001) method. Using SoilMoisture 2710 tensiometers, we measured soil water content for 1 L soil samples across the 0 to -85 kPa range (precision ± 2 kPa). For drier soils, we used a psychrometric technique from Wescor, enabling ± 20 kPa accuracy in water content determination. A function describing the soil water characteristic curve was fitted to these data, and the average soil water content was calculated for each nominal soil water content. The soil samples were left in a cold room at 5 °C to equilibrate for 24 hours in cylindrical plastic pots with airtight lids. Based on the literature, for all experiments, the soil moisture level of 20 % (-2.57 kPa) was defined as the control. This moisture level is ideal for enchytraeids, allowing them to remain fully active without exhibiting any signs of stress (Beylich and Achazi 1999; Maraldo et al., 2008). [SOURCE: Page_73]

Table 3. Nominal and actual soil water content and soil water potential of the soil used in experiment. Values of the two latter parameters are mean ± SE, N=5. [SOURCE: Page_73]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_74]

In order to test the effect of swp on survival, reproduction and body water content, we used plastic beakers (5 cm diameter, 7 cm high) containing 30 g ± 3.0 dry mass soil. Ten adults were placed on the surface of the soil and the beakers were covered with parafilm and closed with a screwcap lids both pierced with a needle to allow gas exchange. Beakers were placed in a climate room at 20 °C ± 0.5 °C and a 12:12 h light/darkness cycle. Enchytraeids were fed ad libitum on a weekly basis by adding 40 mg of finely rolled oats to the surface of the soil. Soil moisture content was checked weekly by weighing and moisture was adjusted if needed. Five experimental replicates per treatment were used through the whole range of 10 different moisture levels. The first incubation period lasted three weeks, during which the adults produced cocoons. [SOURCE: Page_74]

After three weeks, the soil was poured into a tray and the adults were carefully removed from the soil, counted to assess mortality, and gently wiped with a damp piece of filter paper before being transferred to a pre-weighed aluminium tincap. Using featherweight forceps, the tincap was then gently sealed and immediately weighed to the nearest microgram using a microbalance (Sartorius AG, Göttingen, Germany). Subsequently, the adult enchytraeids were dried at 80 °C for 24h to determine their dry weight (including both the tincap and the enchytraeids). Based on these measurements, the average dry weight per animal and their average water content were calculated for each replicate. [SOURCE: Page_74]

Immediately after collection of adults, the soil from which the adults were collected was carefully reassembled in the beakers and incubated for three more weeks, in the same conditions, for cocoon hatching. After the three weeks, juveniles were extracted by the Baermann extraction funnel method for 5 h as described by Dai et al. (2021). The water of the Baermann funnel was emptied into a 200 mL beaker and left for sedimentation overnight at 5 °C, and then the extracted juveniles were counted using a dissection microscope. [SOURCE: Page_74]

2.4. Feeding activity Bait-lamina test (ISO, 2014) was used to assess the feeding activity of enchytraeids. This method usually used in field can be adapted for laboratory conditions (Helling et al., 1998; Bart et al., 2018). The sticks (from Terra Protecta, GmbH, Berlin, Germany) were 18 cm long, 6 mm wide and 1.5 mm thick. The 16 holes spaced 5 mm apart on each stick were filled with bait, a mixture prepared beforehand of 70 % cellulose powder, 25 % finely ground wheat bran and 5 % activated carbon powder (ISO, 2014). In order to test the effect of swp on the feeding activity, we used plastic tubes (20 cm high, 3 [SOURCE: Page_74]

56 [SOURCE: Page_74]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_75]

cm in diameter) containing 150 g ± 15.0 dry mass soil. At half-filled process, the bait filled stick was gently vertically inserted into the soil, and 30 adults were added. The other half of the soil was then added to the container with the uppermost hole in the bait lamina being levelled ca. 0.5 cm below the soil surface. The container was closed with a screwcap lid and pierced with a needle to allow gas exchange. All test containers were placed in a climate room at 20 °C ± 0.5 °C and a 12:12 h light/darkness cycle during a 10-day incubation period. Five experimental replicates per treatment were used across 8 moisture levels, ranging from -28.79 kPa to -1.79 kPa. The two driest conditions were excluded, as our previous reproduction experiments showed that enchytraeids did not survive under those conditions. [SOURCE: Page_75]

At the end of the exposure period, the strips were carefully removed from the soil and gently free of soil or organic particles covering them. A visual assessment was done which involves viewing the strips against the light and counting the empty apertures. The number of empty openings and their vertical location, i.e. between n°1 (depth in the ground = 0.5 cm) and n°16 (depth in the ground = 8.0 cm) were read. The aperture was considered as “pierced” if at least half of the bait was consumed. The total number of “pierced” baits was noted to represent the feeding activity which was calculated as the percentage of the total openings. [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.5. Avoidance test We wished to investigate whether E. albidus can sense dry soil and react by avoidance behavior. To do so we conducted an avoidance test using a modified version of the test described by Amorim et al. (2008a, b). We used plastic circular dish (8 cm diameter × 4 cm height) containing 50g ± 5.0 dry mass soil. Each dish was separated with removable plastic wall into two equal sections during the filling process, and then half filled with soil of the control moisture (-2.57 kPa) and half with the tested modalities through the whole range of 10 different moisture levels (from -236.15 kPa to -1.79 kPa) (Figure 15A). The plastic wall was delicately removed, and ten enchytraeid individuals were placed on the soil contact line, buried 0.5 cm deep (Figure 15B). The vessels were covered with both parafilm and a lid pierced with a needle and were placed in a climate room at 20 °C ± 0.5 °C and a 12:12 h light/darkness cycle during a 24h incubation period. Eight experimental replicates per treatment were made. The day after, the plastic wall was repositioned in the center to separate the compartments with different soil moistures and the enchytraeids present in each compartment were counted. [SOURCE: Page_75]

57 [SOURCE: Page_75]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_76]

Figure 15. (A) Set-up of the avoidance test experiment with (B) a picture of a sample being set up, with the enchytraeids in the centre, at the soil contact line, just before joining the two sections of soil. [SOURCE: Page_76]

2.6. Statistical analysis All the data were plotted on a logarithmic pF scale log(10) as a function of soil water potential (-kPa), i.e. with drought increasing towards the right. For survival and feeding activity test, dose-response curves were fitted to data using the drm function from the drc package. For water content in enchytraeids, a linear curve has been fitted. The avoidance endpoint data was expressed as the percentage of worms that avoided the treated soil in the test container from the total number of worms in that container. The results are the mean percentages of net responses (NR) calculated as follows: 𝐶−𝑇 𝑁𝑅 = (    ) ∗ 100 𝑁 [SOURCE: Page_76]

where 𝐶 is the number of worms observed in the control soil; 𝑇 is the number of worms observed in test soil; 𝑁 is the total number of worms per replicate. A positive (+) NR indicated avoidance and a negative (-) NR indicated a non-response (or attraction) to the chemical or soil tested. [SOURCE: Page_76]

For statistical analysis, one-way ANOVA tests were applied when the conditions for parametric tests were satisfied, including normality of residuals (Lilliefors test) and homogeneity of variances (Bartlett test). When these conditions were not met, logarithmic or square root transformations were attempted. If parametric conditions still could not be satisfied, Kruskal- Wallis tests with Bonferroni adjustment were conducted. For pairwise comparisons between each treatment and the control (-2.57 kPa), Student's t-tests were used when the conditions for parametric tests were met. Otherwise, Wilcoxon rank-sum tests were performed (R Development Core Team, 2014). [SOURCE: Page_76]

58 [SOURCE: Page_76]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_77]

### 3. Results 3.1. Survival

Figure 16. Percentage of surviving adult E. albidus after 3 weeks of incubation at various soil water contents. Dots are means ± SE (N=5), in red the control treatment. A sigmoidal dose-response curve has been fitted to the data. * indicate a significant difference from the control (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_77]

The survival rate remained near 100 % throughout the range of soil water potential from -1.79 to -7.27 kPa. When soil water content decreased beyond -7.27 kPa (14 % gravimetric moisture content), the survival rate declined (Figure 16). From -14.07 kPa and drier, enchytraeid survival was significantly different from the control (Wilcoxon test, p-value <0.05, for the three drier treatments), resulting in 100 % mortality in 11 replicates out of 15 (Figure 16). The water content that killed 50 % of the enchytraieds LWC50 (Lethal Water Content 50) was -17.8 kPa [-16.6; -26.9] [SOURCE: Page_77]

### 3.2. Enchytraeid water content

Figure 17. Body water content (mg mg-1 dry mass) of adult E. albidus after 3 weeks of incubation at various soil water contents, in red the control treatment. The dotted line indicate the mean (± SE; N=5) water content adults of the control treatment. * indicates a significant difference from the control (p<0.05). A linear trend line was fitted to the data (R$_2$=0.55). [SOURCE: Page_78]

Enchytraeids from the control treatment -2.57 kPa contained 2.36 ± 0.26 mg of water mg-1 dry weight. The water content of surviving adults was significantly influenced by the soil water content (ANOVA, p-value = 3.72×10⁻⁴) (Figure 17). At the highest soil moisture of -1.79 kPa, enchytraeids had the maximum water content of 2.74 ± 0.50 mg of water mg-1 dry weight. This value decreased to 1.8 mg of water mg-1 dry weight in the soil with the lowest moisture level - 28.79 kPa. In the driest soil, enchytraeids contained significantly less water than in the control treatment (t-test, p-value = 3.73×10⁻3) (Figure 17). [SOURCE: Page_78]

60 [SOURCE: Page_78]

### 3.3. Reproduction

Figure 18. Number of juvenile E. albidus after 6 weeks of incubation at various soil water content. Dots are means ± SE (N=5), in red the control treatment. * indicates a significant difference from the control (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_79]

The number of juveniles was significantly influenced by soil water content (Kruskal-Wallis test, p-value <0.05) (Figure 18). Compared to the control (-2.57 kPa), significantly fewer juveniles were found in the wettest soil, with 1.6 ± 3.0 juveniles produced during the experiment (Wilcoxon test, p-value = 1.79×10⁻²) (Figure 18). At soil water contents between -2.17 and - 4.83 kPa, juvenile numbers peaked, reaching up to 28 ± 9 juveniles at -3.51 kPa. However, from -14.06 kPa and drier, the number of juveniles significantly decreased (Wilcoxon test, p- value <0.05), stagnating at almost zero (Figure 18). [SOURCE: Page_79]

61 [SOURCE: Page_79]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_80]

### 3.4. Feeding activity – bait lamina test

Figure 19. Feeding activity of adult E. albidus through bait lamina sticks after 10 days of incubation at various soil water contents. Dots are means ± SE (N=5), in red the control treatment. * indicates a significant difference from the control (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_80]

Enchytraeid feeding activity was significantly influenced by soil water content (Kruskal-Wallis test, p-value <0.05) (Figure 19). At moisture levels wetter than -3.51 kPa, enchytraeids exhibited a feeding behavior above 50 % (Figure 19). However, when moisture levels went down to -4.83 kPa, enchytraeid significantly reduce their feeding activity (t-test p-value=0.01), being then significantly different from the control treatment (t-tests p-value <0.05, for the four drier treatment. From -7.27 kPa and drier enchytraeids have completely stopped consuming the baits (Figure 19). [SOURCE: Page_80]

### 3.5. Avoidance

Figure 20. Avoidance net response (%) of adult E. albidus after 24 hours of exposure to different soil water contents (N=5). Dots are means ± SE (N=5), in red the control treatment. * indicates a significant difference from the control (p<0.05). [SOURCE: Page_81]

When tested soil was wetter than -7.27 kPa, E. albidus did not exhibit a significant avoidance behavior (Figure 20). With decreasing soil water content (i.e., at -14.07 and -28.79 kPa), the avoidance percentage increased but remained relatively low (below 50 %) and not significantly different from the control (Figure 20), indicating that moderately dry conditions are still bearable for E. albidus. However, at the two driest moisture, -79.37 and -236.15 kPa, enchytraeids avoided the dry soil (Wilcoxon test, p-values = 1.96×10⁻² and 1.07×10⁻², respectively) (Figure 20). [SOURCE: Page_81]

4. Discussion Through a series of experiments where E. albidus individuals were exposed to a gradient of soil water content, we showed that the threshold for negative effects on life history traits and behavior was around -4.83 kPa, i.e. 16 % in terms of gravimetric moisture content (Table 4). [SOURCE: Page_81]

63 [SOURCE: Page_81]

Chapitre 3 – Les enchytréides face à la sécheresse [SOURCE: Page_82]

Table 4. Responses on avoidance, water content, reproduction, survival and feeding activity (from the least to the most sensitive to drought) of E. albidus to varying soil moisture levels. The green dots are results that are not different from the control (dotted border, the -2.57pKa = 20 % control). The red points are those where the results were significantly different from the control (p<0.05). The slash refers to treatment that did not perform because of a zero enchytraeid survival rate. N is the number of replicates. [SOURCE: Page_82]

Wet soil                                                            Dry soil Saturated pore size     161    133       112    82    60      40     20     10        4         1 Soil water potential range (pKa)           -1.79 - 2.17 - 2.57 - 3.51 - 4.83 - 7.27 - 14.07 - 28.79 - 79.37 - 236.15 Gravimetric moisture content (%)            24     22        20     18    16     14     12     10      8          6 Avoidance       N=8  ●        ●         ●      ●     ●      ●      ●      ●       ●         ● Water content N=5 ●           ●         ●      ●     ●      ●      ●      ●       /          / E. albidus tests   Reproduction N=5 ●            ●         ●      ●     ●      ●      ●      ●       ●         ● Survival         N=5 ●        ●         ●      ●     ●      ●      ●      ●       ●         ● Feeding activity N=5 ●        ●         ●      ●     ●      ●      ●      ●       /          / [SOURCE: Page_82]

The control soil, set at -2.57 kPa, was selected because it is presumed to represent optimal conditions for enchytraeids, which was supported by our findings. At this soil water content, enchytraeids exhibited a 100 % survival rate a good feeding activity (>50 %, i.e. 68 % ± 21 %), and a robust reproductive response (15.6 juveniles ± 10.7 juveniles over 21 days, corresponding to 1.6 juveniles per adult). Moreover, E. albidus water content was 2.36 mg ± 0.26 mg per dry weight, representing 70 % of water in the animal, indicating optimal hydration and physiological health (Maraldo et al., 2009). Additionally, enchytraeids demonstrated a strong preference for this condition during avoidance tests. Slightly more humid soils (-2.17 kPa) also appeared as optimal for enchytraeids, with a 100 % survival rate, a feeding activity of 68 % ± 21 %, and the production of 15.0 juveniles ± 14,1 juveniles. [SOURCE: Page_82]

However, when the soil was wetter (muddy appearance), a significant 90 % reduction in the reproduction was monitored. Some enchytraeids species such as C. sphagnetorum can tolerate very moist environments such as peatlands (Cole et al. 2002a; Briones et. al., 2007; Carrera et al. 2009). However, E. albidus is not adapted to this type of habitat. It typically thrives in loose, non-waterlogged organic matter. In our case, the rapid change in moisture levels, which may have created anaerobic zones, could have triggered a cessation in reproduction. [SOURCE: Page_82]

All the results pointed to the fact that enchytraeids were adversely affected by soil desiccation. From -4.82 kPa and drier, they exhibited a significantly lower feeding activity (8 % ± 8 %). Reproduction is generally considered as the most sensitive endpoint for enchytraeids in 64 [SOURCE: Page_82]
